The article introduces the challenge of fine-tuning large machine learning models on domain-specific, privacy-sensitive data, and motivates the need for differential privacy (DP). It explains the limitations of example-level DP and introduces user-level DP as a stronger privacy guarantee, particularly relevant in federated learning and modern data ownership contexts. - Differential privacy (DP) allows training with privacy guarantees by injecting noise.
- Example-level DP protects individual data points but may not protect users with many examples.
- User-level DP ensures privacy at the user level, preventing attackers from learning whether a user's data was included.
- Datacenter training offers more flexibility than federated learning for implementing user-level DP.

第 1 节

The article introduces the challenge of fine-tuning large machine learning models on domain-specific, privacy-sensitive data, and motivates the need for differential privacy (DP). It explains the limitations of example-level DP and introduces user-level DP as a stronger privacy guarantee, particularly relevant in federated learning and modern data ownership contexts. The section sets the stage for the investigation into scaling user-level DP to large language models (LLMs) in datacenter settings, highlighting the increased flexibility and the central research question: how to leverage this flexibility for better training results.

  1. Fine-tuning ML models on domain-specific data is often necessary but raises privacy concerns.
  2. Differential privacy (DP) allows training with privacy guarantees by injecting noise.
  3. Example-level DP protects individual data points but may not protect users with many examples.
  4. User-level DP ensures privacy at the user level, preventing attackers from learning whether a user's data was included.
  5. Datacenter training offers more flexibility than federated learning for implementing user-level DP.
第 2 节

This section explains the technical foundations of training models with user-level privacy, focusing on the adaptation of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) to differential privacy (DP-SGD). It describes how DP-SGD achieves example-level DP and outlines two main strategies for achieving user-level DP: Example-Level Sampling (ELS) and User-Level Sampling (ULS). The section details the preprocessing step of bounding user contributions and compares the batch formation processes of ELS and ULS, emphasizing the importance of the 'contribution bound' parameter and the need to optimize it for performance.

  1. DP-SGD modifies standard SGD by adding random noise to gradients, enabling privacy.
  2. DP-SGD is effective for example-level DP but needs adaptation for user-level DP.
  3. Two approaches for user-level DP: 1) Apply DP-SGD with more noise (ELS), 2) Sample users to form batches (ULS).
  4. Preprocessing involves bounding the number of examples each user can contribute ('contribution bound').
  5. Optimizing the contribution bound is critical for balancing privacy and model performance.
第 3 节

This section discusses the practical challenges and algorithmic optimizations necessary to make ELS and ULS work effectively for LLM fine-tuning. It highlights the problem of excessive noise in previous approaches and presents new theoretical results that allow for significantly less noise while maintaining privacy guarantees. The section also details strategies for selecting the contribution bound without costly trial-and-error: using the median user example count for ELS and a predictive noise minimization approach for ULS. These optimizations are crucial for making user-level DP feasible and effective in large-scale LLM training.

  1. Previous work added much more noise than necessary for user-level DP; new analysis allows for less noise.
  2. Selecting the contribution bound is essential: median user example count for ELS, predictive minimization for ULS.
  3. Optimizations enable better model quality and practical feasibility for LLM fine-tuning with user-level DP.
第 4 节

This section presents the experimental results comparing the optimized ELS and ULS algorithms on LLM fine-tuning tasks using standard datasets (StackOverflow and CC-News). It demonstrates that the new noise bounds result in exponential reductions in required noise, and that both ELS and ULS outperform pre-trained models under strict privacy constraints. ULS generally performs better, except in scenarios with higher privacy requirements or limited compute. The section provides empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed optimizations.

  1. New theoretical bounds show privacy guarantees decay near-linearly, not exponentially, with contribution bound.
  2. Both ELS and ULS, with optimizations, outperform pre-trained models on user-level DP fine-tuning tasks.
  3. ULS is usually superior, except when higher privacy or less compute is needed.
第 5 节

The article concludes by summarizing the contributions: optimized algorithms (ELS and ULS) for user-level DP in LLM fine-tuning, new privacy guarantees, and practical heuristics for parameter selection. It emphasizes that, despite the strictness of user-level DP and the challenges of large-scale training, the proposed methods make fine-tuning on sensitive datasets both feasible and beneficial, enabling strong user protections without sacrificing model quality.

  1. Optimized ELS and ULS algorithms provide practical, well-justified parameter choices for user-level DP.
  2. Fine-tuning LLMs with user-level DP is feasible and advantageous over using only pre-trained models.
  3. The work enables secure model training on sensitive datasets with strong privacy guarantees.

33123a17f9bb3d65a144cb05ca086174

Example-level Differential Privacy (DP). DP (Dwork et al., 2006) gives provable bounds on privacy leakage. We study how example-level DP, which protects the privacy of individual examples, impacts user inference. We train the 125M model on Enron Emails using DP-Adam, a variant of Adam that clips per-example gradients and adds noise calibrated to the privacy budget $\varepsilon$ . We find next that example-level DP can somewhat mitigate user inference while incurring increased compute cost and a degraded model utility.

User-level differential privacy (DP) is a relatively recent and less-explored area compared to the more established item-level DP setting. It has gained increased attention lately due to its significance in machine learning applications, particularly in the context of federated learning. Several works have studied user-level DP for several applications, including DPSCO [Levy et al., 2021, Bassily and Sun, 2023], PAC learning [Badih et al., 2021], and discrete distribution estimation [Liu et al., 2020, Acharya et al., 2023]. In particular, the lower bound in [Levy et al., 2021] shows the tightness of the dependence on $m$ in our results. In recent work, [Ghazi et al., 2023a] proposed a generic transformation of any item-level DP algorithm to a user-level DP algorithm. However, it is inefficient, and the dependence on $\varepsilon$ may not be optimal.

User-level differential privacy is a stronger privacy notion that addresses the above shortcoming of item-level DP. Informally, user-level DP ensures that the inclusion or exclusion of any one user’s entire training data ( $\textbar { m }$ samples) has a negligible impact on the model’s outputs. Thus, user-level DP provides a strong guarantee that no user’s data can be leaked, even when users contribute many training examples.

In this work, we are interested in scalable and practical approaches to fine-tuning LLMs with user-level DP.

However, when users can contribute multiple examples to the training dataset, example-level DP is not necessarily strong enough to ensure the users’ data isn't memorized. Instead, we have designed algorithms for _user-level_ DP, which requires that the output distribution of models doesn't change even if we add/remove _all_ of the training examples from any one user (or all the examples from any one device in our application). [User-level DP]() is a stronger form of privacy that guarantees that an attacker who uses a model can’t learn things about the user, like whether or not the user’s data is included in the training dataset. User-level DP better reflects how data is actually owned in today’s society. It’s used frequently in [federated learning]() to train models across distributed devices like cell phones. Those devices often have many examples, all owned by a single user, and require more stringent privacy guarantees.

8846c61aec8400b7b13c6f91b454209d

We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel userlevel DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLMcompatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.

To remedy this issue, we consider user-level DP, which instead of guaranteeing privacy for individual samples, protects a user’s entire contribution $\textcircled { m } \geq 1$ samples). This is a more stringent but more realistic privacy desideratum. To hold, it requires that the output of our algorithm does not significantly change when changing user’s entire contribution—i.e. possibly swapping up to $m$ samples in total. We make this formal in Definition 1. Very recently, for the reasons outlined above, there has been increasing interest in user-level DP for applications such as estimating discrete distributions under user-level privacy constraints [46], PAC learning with user-level privacy [31], and bounding user contributions in ML models [4, 26]. Differentially private SQL with bounded user contributions was proposed in [59]. User-level privacy has been also studied in the context of learning models via federated learning [49, 48, 58, 6].
txt11 · 检索分 0.770

10054bfedfeb44834a158c4df7f46d6a

Differential Privacy (DP) [23] is an extensively used quantifiable measurement of privacy risk, now generally accepted as a standard notion of privacy in both industry and government [5,18,50,70]. Applied to machine learning, DP requires a training procedure with explicit randomness, and guarantees that the distribution over output models is quantifiably similar given a certain scope of change to the training dataset. A DP guarantee with respect to the change of a single arbitrary training example is known as examplelevel $D P$ ,which provides plausible deniability (in the binary hypothesis testing sense of [38]) that any single example (e.g.,image) occurred in the training dataset. If we instead consider how the distribution of output models changes if the data (including even the number of examples) from any single user change arbitrarily,we have user-level $D P$ [22]. This ensures model training is quantifiably insensitive to all of the data from any one user,and hence it is impossible to tell if a user has participated in training with high confidence. This guarantee can be exponentially stronger than example-level DP if one user may contribute many examples to training.

> Abstract:We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel user-level DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLM-compatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.
txt13 · 检索分 0.766

38921ea1b5d2ed64cede10ebfcb946fc

We propose and analyze algorithms to solve a range of learning tasks under userlevel differential privacy constraints. Rather than guaranteeing only the privacy of individual samples, user-level DP protects a user’s entire contribution $m \geq 1$ samples), providing more stringent but more realistic protection against information leaks. We show that for high-dimensional mean estimation, empirical risk minimization with smooth losses, stochastic convex optimization, and learning hypothesis classes with finite metric entropy, the privacy cost decreases as $O ( 1 / \bar { \sqrt { m } } )$ as users provide more samples. In contrast, when increasing the number of users $n$ , the privacy cost decreases at a faster $O ( 1 / n )$ rate. We complement these results with lower bounds showing the minimax optimality of our algorithms for mean estimation and stochastic convex optimization. Our algorithms rely on novel techniques for private mean estimation in arbitrary dimension with error scaling as the concentration radius $\tau$ of the distribution rather than the entire range.
txt14 · 检索分 0.764

d2885399dd8b8d28c3de756f79634207

Differential privacy is a formal mathematical framework for quantifying how well a data analysis algorithm (typically called a *mechanism*) preserves the privacy of users who contribute to the input dataset. When analyzing a mechanism within the framework of differential privacy, one considers two scenarios from the perspective of a single user — the user’s data is either present in the input dataset or it is absent. The mechanism is said to be private if its output is very similar in each of these scenarios. In the context of ML, the output can be a model, or, as in our case, synthetic data.

Differential privacy (DP) [9] is a promising approach to mitigating these privacy concerns: it gives rigorous guarantees on privacy leakage and can effectively eliminate data leakage from LLMs [10]. DP is most commonly used to protect individual examples in the dataset. This is referred to as example-level or item-level DP. However, in the context of fine-tuning with user data, each user might contribute multiple correlated examples with similarities in language use, vocabulary, and contexts.

Differential Privacy (DP) [23] is an extensively used quantifiable measurement of privacy risk, now generally accepted as a standard notion of privacy in both industry and government [5,18,50,70]. Applied to machine learning, DP requires a training procedure with explicit randomness, and guarantees that the distribution over output models is quantifiably similar given a certain scope of change to the training dataset. A DP guarantee with respect to the change of a single arbitrary training example is known as examplelevel $D P$ ,which provides plausible deniability (in the binary hypothesis testing sense of [38]) that any single example (e.g.,image) occurred in the training dataset. If we instead consider how the distribution of output models changes if the data (including even the number of examples) from any single user change arbitrarily,we have user-level $D P$ [22]. This ensures model training is quantifiably insensitive to all of the data from any one user,and hence it is impossible to tell if a user has participated in training with high confidence. This guarantee can be exponentially stronger than example-level DP if one user may contribute many examples to training.

c1da2de4fbc716519517ca3ca88ec71d

Given the practical importance of user-level DP SCO, it is unsurprising that many prior works have studied this problem. The work of $[ \mathrm { L S A } ^ { + } 2 1 ]$ initiated this line of work, and provided an excess risk lower bound of $\Omega ( 1 / \sqrt { n m } + \sqrt { d } / ( \varepsilon n \sqrt { m } ) )$ , where $\varepsilon$ is the privacy parameter. However, their upper bound was suboptimal and required strong assumptions. The work of [BS23] gave an algorithm√ that achieves optimal risk for √ $\beta$ -smooth losses with √ √ $\beta \ < \ ( n / \sqrt { m d } \land n ^ { 3 / 2 } / ( d \sqrt { m } ) )$ , provided that $n \geq { \sqrt { d } } / \varepsilon$ and $m \leq \operatorname* { m a x } ( \sqrt { d } , n \varepsilon ^ { 2 } / \sqrt { d } )$ . These assumptions are restrictive in large-scale applications with a large number of examples per user $m$ or when the number of model parameters $d$ is large. For example, in deep learning, we often have $d \gg n$ and an enormous smoothness parameter $\beta \gg 1$ . Moreover, their algorithm requires $m n ^ { 3 / 2 }$ gradient evaluations, making it slow when the number of users $n$ is large.2 The work of $[ \mathrm { G K K ^ { + } } 2 3 ]$ gave another user-level DP algorithm that only requires $n \geq \log ( d ) / \varepsilon$ , but unfortunately their algorithm does not run in polynomial-time.

Machine learning models should not reveal particular information that is not otherwise accessible. Differential privacy provides a formal framework to mitigate privacy risks by ensuring that the inclusion or exclusion of any single data point does not significantly alter the output of an algorithm, thus limiting the exposure of private information. This survey paper explores the foundational ... [Show full abstract] definitions of differential privacy, reviews its original formulations and tracing its evolution through key research contributions. It then provides an in-depth examination of how DP has been integrated into machine learning models, analyzing existing proposals and methods to preserve privacy when training ML models. Finally, it describes how DP-based ML techniques can be evaluated in practice. %Finally, it discusses the broader implications of DP, highlighting its potential for public benefit, its real-world applications, and the challenges it faces, including vulnerabilities to adversarial attacks. By offering a comprehensive overview of differential privacy in machine learning, this work aims to contribute to the ongoing development of secure and responsible AI systems.

ad4e50c3285fccf7ee94fcdad474e23f

Differential Privacy provides an invaluable standard for reasoning about and quantifying privacy loss in an era of large-scale data analysis and machine learning. In machine learning, DP has been successfully adapted to a wide range of algorithms. While objective perturbation is common for convex models like SVMs, and specialized techniques exist for decision trees and ensembles, gradient perturbation via DP-SGD has become the de facto standard for deep learning. This involves careful gradient clipping, noise addition, and sophisticated privacy accounting over iterative training.

### Coarse-grained Description:
The image illustrates the difference between example-level differential privacy (DP) and user-level DP in the context of machine learning models. It compares how datasets are processed and trained under these two privacy settings, emphasizing the distinctions in data aggregation and training.

### Fine-grained Description:

#### Example-level DP:
- **Datasets:**
  - Three users (User 1, User 2, User 3) contribute different data points.
  - User 1 contributes triangles, User 2 contributes circles, and User 3 contributes diamonds.
  - The datasets show that each user's examples are individually included.
- **Training Process:**
  - Each dataset undergoes "Noisy Training" to ensure privacy.
  - The resulting models are identical, indicating that the noise addition process does not affect the model outcome despite differences in the datasets.

#### User-level DP:
- **Datasets:**
  - Similar structure with three users contributing different shapes.
  - However, the right side shows a blue box covering User 1's data, indicating aggregation or exclusion in user-level DP.
- **Training Process:**
  - Datasets undergo "Noisy Training," treating each user's data as a group.
  - The resulting models are identical, suggesting that the privacy mechanism at the user level maintains consistency despite aggregated data handling.

#### General Observations:
- **Models:**
  - Both scenarios result in models that "look the same," highlighting the effectiveness of DP in maintaining model performance while ensuring privacy.
- **Privacy Levels:**
  - Example-level DP focuses on individual data points, while user-level DP considers entire user datasets, emphasizing different privacy granularities.

  - Similar structure with three users contributing different shapes.

  - Datasets undergo "Noisy Training," treating each user's data as a group.
  - The resulting models are identical, suggesting that the privacy mechanism at the user level maintains consistency despite aggregated data handling.

- **Privacy Levels:**
  - Example-level DP focuses on individual data points, while user-level DP considers entire user datasets, emphasizing different privacy granularities.

It explains the limitations of example-level DP and introduces user-level DP as a stronger privacy guarantee, particularly relevant in federated learning and modern data ownership contexts. - Fine-tuning ML models on domain-specific data is often necessary but raises privacy concerns.
- Differential privacy (DP) allows training with privacy guarantees by injecting noise.
- Example-level DP protects individual data points but may not protect users with many examples.
- User-level DP ensures privacy at the user level, preventing attackers from learning whether a user's data was included.
- Datacenter training offers more flexibility than federated learning for implementing user-level DP.

[Source txt1 begin]
Example-level Differential Privacy (DP). DP (Dwork et al., 2006) gives provable bounds on privacy leakage. We study how example-level DP, which protects the privacy of individual examples, impacts user inference. We train the 125M model on Enron Emails using DP-Adam, a variant of Adam that clips per-example gradients and adds noise calibrated to the privacy budget $\varepsilon$ . We find next that example-level DP can somewhat mitigate user inference while incurring increased compute cost and a degraded model utility.
[Source txt1 end]

[Source txt2 begin]

[Source txt3 begin]
User-level differential privacy (DP) is a relatively recent and less-explored area compared to the more established item-level DP setting. It has gained increased attention lately due to its significance in machine learning applications, particularly in the context of federated learning. Several works have studied user-level DP for several applications, including DPSCO [Levy et al., 2021, Bassily and Sun, 2023], PAC learning [Badih et al., 2021], and discrete distribution estimation [Liu et al., 2020, Acharya et al., 2023]. In particular, the lower bound in [Levy et al., 2021] shows the tightness of the dependence on $m$ in our results. In recent work, [Ghazi et al., 2023a] proposed a generic transformation of any item-level DP algorithm to a user-level DP algorithm. However, it is inefficient, and the dependence on $\varepsilon$ may not be optimal.
[Source txt3 end]

[Source txt4 begin]
1. We apply differential privacy to model training using the notion of user-adjacent datasets, leading to formal guarantees of user-level privacy, rather than privacy for single examples.
[Source txt4 end]

[Source txt5 begin]
User-level differential privacy is a stronger privacy notion that addresses the above shortcoming of item-level DP. Informally, user-level DP ensures that the inclusion or exclusion of any one user’s entire training data ( $\textbar { m }$ samples) has a negligible impact on the model’s outputs. Thus, user-level DP provides a strong guarantee that no user’s data can be leaked, even when users contribute many training examples.
[Source txt5 end]

[Source txt6 begin]
Thus, example-level DP can fail to adequately protect a user’s privacy from, for instance, membership inference attacks at the user-level [11, 12]. However, DP can also be instantiated at the user level to safeguard all the data contributed by a user and provably mitigate such concerns. In this work, we are interested in scalable and practical approaches to fine-tuning LLMs with user-level DP.
[Source txt6 end]

[Source txt7 begin]
However, when users can contribute multiple examples to the training dataset, example-level DP is not necessarily strong enough to ensure the users’ data isn't memorized. Instead, we have designed algorithms for _user-level_ DP, which requires that the output distribution of models doesn't change even if we add/remove _all_ of the training examples from any one user (or all the examples from any one device in our application). Fortunately, because FL summarizes all of a user's training data as a single model update, federated algorithms are well-suited to offering user-level DP guarantees.
[Source txt7 end]

[Source txt8 begin]
[User-level DP]() is a stronger form of privacy that guarantees that an attacker who uses a model can’t learn things about the user, like whether or not the user’s data is included in the training dataset. User-level DP better reflects how data is actually owned in today’s society. It’s used frequently in [federated learning]() to train models across distributed devices like cell phones. Those devices often have many examples, all owned by a single user, and require more stringent privacy guarantees.
[Source txt8 end]

[Source txt9 begin]
We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel userlevel DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLMcompatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.
[Source txt9 end]

[Source txt10 begin]
To remedy this issue, we consider user-level DP, which instead of guaranteeing privacy for individual samples, protects a user’s entire contribution $\textcircled { m } \geq 1$ samples). This is a more stringent but more realistic privacy desideratum. To hold, it requires that the output of our algorithm does not significantly change when changing user’s entire contribution—i.e. possibly swapping up to $m$ samples in total. We make this formal in Definition 1. Very recently, for the reasons outlined above, there has been increasing interest in user-level DP for applications such as estimating discrete distributions under user-level privacy constraints [46], PAC learning with user-level privacy [31], and bounding user contributions in ML models [4, 26]. Differentially private SQL with bounded user contributions was proposed in [59]. User-level privacy has been also studied in the context of learning models via federated learning [49, 48, 58, 6].
[Source txt10 end]

[Source txt11 begin]
Differential Privacy (DP) [23] is an extensively used quantifiable measurement of privacy risk, now generally accepted as a standard notion of privacy in both industry and government [5,18,50,70]. Applied to machine learning, DP requires a training procedure with explicit randomness, and guarantees that the distribution over output models is quantifiably similar given a certain scope of change to the training dataset. A DP guarantee with respect to the change of a single arbitrary training example is known as examplelevel $D P$ ,which provides plausible deniability (in the binary hypothesis testing sense of [38]) that any single example (e.g.,image) occurred in the training dataset. If we instead consider how the distribution of output models changes if the data (including even the number of examples) from any single user change arbitrarily,we have user-level $D P$ [22]. This ensures model training is quantifiably insensitive to all of the data from any one user,and hence it is impossible to tell if a user has participated in training with high confidence. This guarantee can be exponentially stronger than example-level DP if one user may contribute many examples to training.
[Source txt11 end]

[Source txt12 begin]
> Abstract:We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel user-level DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLM-compatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.
[Source txt12 end]

[Source txt13 begin]
We propose and analyze algorithms to solve a range of learning tasks under userlevel differential privacy constraints. Rather than guaranteeing only the privacy of individual samples, user-level DP protects a user’s entire contribution $m \geq 1$ samples), providing more stringent but more realistic protection against information leaks. We show that for high-dimensional mean estimation, empirical risk minimization with smooth losses, stochastic convex optimization, and learning hypothesis classes with finite metric entropy, the privacy cost decreases as $O ( 1 / \bar { \sqrt { m } } )$ as users provide more samples. In contrast, when increasing the number of users $n$ , the privacy cost decreases at a faster $O ( 1 / n )$ rate. We complement these results with lower bounds showing the minimax optimality of our algorithms for mean estimation and stochastic convex optimization. Our algorithms rely on novel techniques for private mean estimation in arbitrary dimension with error scaling as the concentration radius $\tau$ of the distribution rather than the entire range.
[Source txt13 end]

[Source txt14 begin]
Differential privacy is a formal mathematical framework for quantifying how well a data analysis algorithm (typically called a *mechanism*) preserves the privacy of users who contribute to the input dataset. When analyzing a mechanism within the framework of differential privacy, one considers two scenarios from the perspective of a single user — the user’s data is either present in the input dataset or it is absent. The mechanism is said to be private if its output is very similar in each of these scenarios. In the context of ML, the output can be a model, or, as in our case, synthetic data.
[Source txt14 end]

[Source txt15 begin]
Differential privacy (DP) [9] is a promising approach to mitigating these privacy concerns: it gives rigorous guarantees on privacy leakage and can effectively eliminate data leakage from LLMs [10]. DP is most commonly used to protect individual examples in the dataset. This is referred to as example-level or item-level DP. However, in the context of fine-tuning with user data, each user might contribute multiple correlated examples with similarities in language use, vocabulary, and contexts.
[Source txt15 end]

[Source txt16 begin]
Differential Privacy (DP) [23] is an extensively used quantifiable measurement of privacy risk, now generally accepted as a standard notion of privacy in both industry and government [5,18,50,70]. Applied to machine learning, DP requires a training procedure with explicit randomness, and guarantees that the distribution over output models is quantifiably similar given a certain scope of change to the training dataset. A DP guarantee with respect to the change of a single arbitrary training example is known as examplelevel $D P$ ,which provides plausible deniability (in the binary hypothesis testing sense of [38]) that any single example (e.g.,image) occurred in the training dataset. If we instead consider how the distribution of output models changes if the data (including even the number of examples) from any single user change arbitrarily,we have user-level $D P$ [22]. This ensures model training is quantifiably insensitive to all of the data from any one user,and hence it is impossible to tell if a user has participated in training with high confidence. This guarantee can be exponentially stronger than example-level DP if one user may contribute many examples to training.
[Source txt16 end]

[Source txt17 begin]
Given the practical importance of user-level DP SCO, it is unsurprising that many prior works have studied this problem. The work of $[ \mathrm { L S A } ^ { + } 2 1 ]$ initiated this line of work, and provided an excess risk lower bound of $\Omega ( 1 / \sqrt { n m } + \sqrt { d } / ( \varepsilon n \sqrt { m } ) )$ , where $\varepsilon$ is the privacy parameter. However, their upper bound was suboptimal and required strong assumptions. The work of [BS23] gave an algorithm√ that achieves optimal risk for √ $\beta$ -smooth losses with √ √ $\beta \ < \ ( n / \sqrt { m d } \land n ^ { 3 / 2 } / ( d \sqrt { m } ) )$ , provided that $n \geq { \sqrt { d } } / \varepsilon$ and $m \leq \operatorname* { m a x } ( \sqrt { d } , n \varepsilon ^ { 2 } / \sqrt { d } )$ . These assumptions are restrictive in large-scale applications with a large number of examples per user $m$ or when the number of model parameters $d$ is large. For example, in deep learning, we often have $d \gg n$ and an enormous smoothness parameter $\beta \gg 1$ . Moreover, their algorithm requires $m n ^ { 3 / 2 }$ gradient evaluations, making it slow when the number of users $n$ is large.2 The work of $[ \mathrm { G K K ^ { + } } 2 3 ]$ gave another user-level DP algorithm that only requires $n \geq \log ( d ) / \varepsilon$ , but unfortunately their algorithm does not run in polynomial-time.
[Source txt17 end]

[Source txt18 begin]
1. We apply differential privacy to model training using the notion of user-adjacent datasets, leading to formal guarantees of user-level privacy, rather than privacy for single examples.
[Source txt18 end]

[Source txt19 begin]
Machine learning models should not reveal particular information that is not otherwise accessible. Differential privacy provides a formal framework to mitigate privacy risks by ensuring that the inclusion or exclusion of any single data point does not significantly alter the output of an algorithm, thus limiting the exposure of private information. This survey paper explores the foundational ... [Show full abstract] definitions of differential privacy, reviews its original formulations and tracing its evolution through key research contributions. It then provides an in-depth examination of how DP has been integrated into machine learning models, analyzing existing proposals and methods to preserve privacy when training ML models. Finally, it describes how DP-based ML techniques can be evaluated in practice. %Finally, it discusses the broader implications of DP, highlighting its potential for public benefit, its real-world applications, and the challenges it faces, including vulnerabilities to adversarial attacks. By offering a comprehensive overview of differential privacy in machine learning, this work aims to contribute to the ongoing development of secure and responsible AI systems.
[Source txt19 end]

[Source txt20 begin]
Differential Privacy provides an invaluable standard for reasoning about and quantifying privacy loss in an era of large-scale data analysis and machine learning. In machine learning, DP has been successfully adapted to a wide range of algorithms. While objective perturbation is common for convex models like SVMs, and specialized techniques exist for decision trees and ensembles, gradient perturbation via DP-SGD has become the de facto standard for deep learning. This involves careful gradient clipping, noise addition, and sophisticated privacy accounting over iterative training.
[Source txt20 end]

You MUST follow these citation rules strictly:

1. **Text Citation Format:**
   - When using information from a text source, cite it as: `[citation:txt1]`, `[citation:txt2]`, etc.
   - Example: "Recent studies show significant progress in AI safety[citation:txt3]."

2. **Image Citation Format:**
   - When you want to insert an image/figure, use: `![](citation:img1)`, `![](citation:img2)`, etc.
   - Example: "The architecture is shown below:
![](citation:img5)
As illustrated, the system..."
   - ONLY use this format for sources explicitly marked as "Image"

3. **Citation Placement:**
   - Place text citations at the end of the sentence or claim
   - Place image citations on their own line where the visual should appear
   - You can cite the same source multiple times if needed

4. **Important Distinctions:**
   - Text sources → use `[citation:txt1]`, `[citation:txt2]`, etc.
   - Image sources → use `![](citation:img1)`, `![](citation:img2)`, etc.

模型原始输出
# The Challenge of Fine-Tuning Models on Privacy-Sensitive Data

Fine-tuning large machine learning models on domain-specific data is a common and powerful practice that enables models to better adapt to specialized tasks and industries. However, this process often involves sensitive user data, raising significant privacy concerns. As models are trained on personal or proprietary data, there is a risk that the model might inadvertently memorize or leak private information, which can be exploited through attacks like membership inference or model inversion [citation:txt6]. This has led to a growing demand for privacy-preserving techniques that allow models to learn from data without compromising the confidentiality of individual users.

Differential privacy (DP) offers a rigorous and mathematically grounded framework for achieving this goal. By introducing calibrated noise into the training process, DP ensures that the output of a model remains statistically similar regardless of whether any one individual’s data is included or excluded [citation:txt11]. This guarantees a formal privacy bound, typically quantified by a privacy budget denoted as $\varepsilon$. In the context of machine learning, DP is often implemented through methods like DP-SGD (Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent), which involves gradient clipping and noise addition to maintain privacy while preserving model utility [citation:txt20].

Despite its promise, traditional DP approaches often operate at the example level, where each individual data point is protected. While this can offer some privacy guarantees, it falls short in scenarios where a single user contributes multiple examples to the training set. For instance, in federated learning or when fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), users may contribute extensive and correlated data. In such cases, example-level DP may not provide sufficient protection, as an attacker could still infer a user’s participation by analyzing the collective patterns in the model’s outputs [citation:txt7].

User-level differential privacy addresses this limitation by offering a stronger privacy guarantee. Instead of protecting individual examples, user-level DP ensures that the inclusion or exclusion of an entire user’s data has a negligible impact on the model. This is particularly important in modern data ecosystems, where users often contribute multiple, potentially sensitive examples, and where data ownership is increasingly centralized in datacenter settings [citation:txt8]. By treating each user’s data as a single unit, user-level DP prevents the model from being overly influenced by any one user’s contribution, thereby reducing the risk of privacy breaches [citation:txt5].

Implementing user-level DP in datacenter environments offers greater flexibility compared to federated learning, where communication and computational constraints often limit the scope of privacy-preserving techniques. This flexibility allows for the development of more sophisticated and scalable algorithms tailored for large-scale LLM fine-tuning. The central research question this article investigates is how to leverage this flexibility to design effective user-level DP algorithms that maintain strong privacy guarantees while minimizing the trade-offs in model performance and computational cost.
查看生成的正文

Fine-tuning large machine learning models on domain-specific data is a common and powerful practice that enables models to better adapt to specialized tasks and industries. However, this process often involves sensitive user data, raising significant privacy concerns. As models are trained on personal or proprietary data, there is a risk that the model might inadvertently memorize or leak private information, which can be exploited through attacks like membership inference or model inversion [txt6]. This has led to a growing demand for privacy-preserving techniques that allow models to learn from data without compromising the confidentiality of individual users.

Differential privacy (DP) offers a rigorous and mathematically grounded framework for achieving this goal. By introducing calibrated noise into the training process, DP ensures that the output of a model remains statistically similar regardless of whether any one individual’s data is included or excluded [txt11]. This guarantees a formal privacy bound, typically quantified by a privacy budget denoted as $\varepsilon$. In the context of machine learning, DP is often implemented through methods like DP-SGD (Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent), which involves gradient clipping and noise addition to maintain privacy while preserving model utility [txt20].

Despite its promise, traditional DP approaches often operate at the example level, where each individual data point is protected. While this can offer some privacy guarantees, it falls short in scenarios where a single user contributes multiple examples to the training set. For instance, in federated learning or when fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), users may contribute extensive and correlated data. In such cases, example-level DP may not provide sufficient protection, as an attacker could still infer a user’s participation by analyzing the collective patterns in the model’s outputs [txt7].

User-level differential privacy addresses this limitation by offering a stronger privacy guarantee. Instead of protecting individual examples, user-level DP ensures that the inclusion or exclusion of an entire user’s data has a negligible impact on the model. This is particularly important in modern data ecosystems, where users often contribute multiple, potentially sensitive examples, and where data ownership is increasingly centralized in datacenter settings [txt8]. By treating each user’s data as a single unit, user-level DP prevents the model from being overly influenced by any one user’s contribution, thereby reducing the risk of privacy breaches [txt5].

Implementing user-level DP in datacenter environments offers greater flexibility compared to federated learning, where communication and computational constraints often limit the scope of privacy-preserving techniques. This flexibility allows for the development of more sophisticated and scalable algorithms tailored for large-scale LLM fine-tuning. The central research question this article investigates is how to leverage this flexibility to design effective user-level DP algorithms that maintain strong privacy guarantees while minimizing the trade-offs in model performance and computational cost.

第 2 节

Technical Foundations of User-Level Privacy in Model Training

本节描述与检查清单

This section explains the technical foundations of training models with user-level privacy, focusing on the adaptation of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) to differential privacy (DP-SGD). It describes how DP-SGD achieves example-level DP and outlines two main strategies for achieving user-level DP: Example-Level Sampling (ELS) and User-Level Sampling (ULS). The section details the preprocessing step of bounding user contributions and compares the batch formation processes of ELS and ULS, emphasizing the importance of the 'contribution bound' parameter and the need to optimize it for performance.

  1. DP-SGD modifies standard SGD by adding random noise to gradients, enabling privacy.
  2. DP-SGD is effective for example-level DP but needs adaptation for user-level DP.
  3. Two approaches for user-level DP: 1) Apply DP-SGD with more noise (ELS), 2) Sample users to form batches (ULS).
  4. Preprocessing involves bounding the number of examples each user can contribute ('contribution bound').
  5. Optimizing the contribution bound is critical for balancing privacy and model performance.

Content: # The Challenge of Fine-Tuning Models on Privacy-Sensitive Data

Fine-tuning large machine learning...


**Current Section Description:**
This section explains the technical foundations of training models with user-level privacy, focusing on the adaptation of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) to differential privacy (DP-SGD). It describes how DP-SGD achieves example-level DP and outlines two main strategies for achieving user-level DP: Example-Level Sampling (ELS) and User-Level Sampling (ULS). The section details the preprocessing step of bounding user contributions and compares the batch formation processes of ELS and ULS, emphasizing the importance of the 'contribution bound' parameter and the need to optimize it for performance.

**Current Section Requirements:**
- DP-SGD modifies standard SGD by adding random noise to gradients, enabling privacy.
- DP-SGD is effective for example-level DP but needs adaptation for user-level DP.
- Two approaches for user-level DP: 1) Apply DP-SGD with more noise (ELS), 2) Sample users to form batches (ULS).
- Preprocessing involves bounding the number of examples each user can contribute ('contribution bound').
- Optimizing the contribution bound is critical for balancing privacy and model performance.

3.4.1 Differentially-Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD). Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD) [1] adapts SGD to satisfy $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -differential privacy by clipping per-example gradients to a fixed norm and adding Gaussian noise. This method builds on stochastic optimization methods [189] [128] [190] to ensure differential privacy in deep learning.
txt2 · 检索分 0.821

cceed32298aa04aeca58daac79802f8b

A differentially private version of stochastic gradient descent useful for training ML models can be obtained by bounding the influence of any individual sample in the trained model and masking it with noise. The resulting algorithm is called DP-SGD (Abadi et al., 2016) and proceeds by iteratively updating parameters with a privatized gradient descent step. Given a sampling probability $q$ , current model parameters $\theta$ and a loss function $\ell ( \theta , \cdot )$ , the privatized gradient $g$ is obtained by first creating a mini-batch $B$ including each point in the training dataset with probability $q$ , summing the $L _ { 2 }$ -clipped gradients3 for each point in $B$ , and adding Gaussian noise with standard deviation $\sigma \cdot C$ to all coordinates of the gradient: $\begin{array} { r } { g \gets \sum _ { z \in B } \mathrm { c l i p } _ { C } \left( \nabla _ { \theta } \ell ( \theta , z ) \right) + \mathcal { N } ( 0 , C ^ { 2 } \sigma ^ { 2 } I ) } \end{array}$ . Running DP-SGD for T training steps yields an (ϵ, δ)-DP mechanism with ϵ ≈ q T log(1/δ)σ (Abadi et al., 2016) – in practice, tighter numerical bounds on $\epsilon$ are often used (Gopi et al., 2021; Google DP Team, 2022). For analytic purposes it is often useful to consider alternatives to $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -DP to capture the differences between distributions $M ( D )$ and $M ( D ^ { \prime } )$ . Renyi differential privacy (RDP) (Mironov, 2017) is one ´ such alternative often used in the context of DP-SGD. It states that the mechanism is $( \alpha , \epsilon )$ -RDP for $\alpha > 1$ and $\epsilon \geq 0$ if $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } _ { W \sim M ( D ^ { \prime } ) } \left[ \left( \frac { \mathbb { P } [ M ( D ) = W ] } { \mathbb { P } [ M ( D ^ { \prime } ) = W ] } \right) ^ { \alpha } \right] \le e ^ { ( \alpha - 1 ) \epsilon } } \end{array}$ . In particular, $T$ iterations of full-batch DP-SGD (i.e. $q = 1$ ) with noise multiplier $\sigma$ give $\textstyle \left( \alpha , { \frac { \alpha T } { 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } } } \right)$ -RDP for every $\alpha > 1$ .
txt3 · 检索分 0.820

8846c61aec8400b7b13c6f91b454209d

We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel userlevel DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLMcompatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.

33a082b24911051b07cf0f8c1d408396

**Abstract :** Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for tackling complex tasks across diverse domains, but they also raise privacy concerns when fine-tuned on sensitive data due to potential memorization. While differential privacy (DP) offers a promising solution by ensuring models are “almost indistinguishable” with or without any particular privacy unit, current evaluations on LLMs mostly treat each example (text record) as the privacy unit. This leads to uneven user privacy guarantees when contributions per user vary. We therefore study user-level DP motivated by applications where it is necessary to ensure uniform privacy protection across users. We present a systematic evaluation of user-level DP for LLM fine-tuning on natural language generation tasks. Focusing on two mechanisms for achieving user-level DP guarantees, Group Privacy and User-wise DP-SGD, we investigate design choices like data selection strategies and parameter tuning for the best privacy-utility tradeoff.
txt9 · 检索分 0.803

7b9bd7c4aa4a43c6d38fc54959578e6c

Differential privacy and DP-SGD. Differential privacy (Dwork et al., 2006) formalizes the idea that data analysis algorithms whose output does not overly depend on any individual input data point can provide reasonable privacy protections. Formally, we say that a randomized mechanism $M$ satisfies $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -differential privacy (DP) if, for any two datasets $D , D ^ { \prime }$ that differ by one point, and any subset $S$ of the output space we have $P [ M ( \dot { D } ) \in S ] \leq e ^ { \epsilon } P [ M ( D ^ { \prime } ) \in S ] + \delta$ . Informally, this means that DP mechanisms bound evidence an adversary can collect (after observing the output) about whether the point where $D$ and $D ^ { \prime }$ differ was used in the analysis. For the bound to provide a meaningful protection against an adversary interested in this membership question it is necessary to take $\epsilon \approx 1$ and $\delta \leq 1 / | D |$ .

txt11 · 检索分 0.791

ffbf239e6fe82e03dbebd1b09fa4d784

Differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DPSGD) (Song et al., 2013; Bassily et al., 2014; Abadi et al., 2016) has become state-of-the-art in training private (deep) learning models (Abadi et al., 2016; McMahan et al., 2018; Erlingsson et al., 2020; Papernot et al., 2020b; Facebook, 2020; Tramer & Boneh \` , 2021). It operates by running stochastic gradient descent (Robbins & Monro, 1951) on noisy mini-batch gradients1, with the noise calibrated such that it ensures differential privacy. The privacy analysis heavily uses tools like privacy amplification by sampling/shuffling (Kasiviswanathan et al., 2008; Bassily et al., 2014; Abadi et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2019; Zhu & Wang, 2019; Erlingsson et al., 2019; Feldman et al., 2020b) to obtain the best privacy/utility trade-offs. Such amplification tools require that each mini-batch is a perfectly (uniformly) random subset of the training data. This assumption can make practical deployment prohibitively hard, especially in the context of distributed settings like federated learning (FL) where one has little control on which subset of the training data one sees at any time (Kairouz et al., 2019; Balle et al., 2020).

Training With Differential Privacy. Differential privacy (DP) [13, 14] is a well-established notion of privacy that offers strong guarantees on the privacy of individual records in the training dataset. Private machine learning models can be trained with variants of the differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DP-SGD) algorithm [1] which is widely implemented [17, 25]. Large companies have even used DP in production machine learning models to protect users’ sensitive information [15,69]. The tradeoffs between privacy and utility of models have been studied extensively: differentially-private training typically prevents models from capturing the long tails of the data distribution and thus hurts utility [19, 20, 67].

An ideal DP algorithm protects the data privacy with the given \\((\epsilon,\delta )\\) guarantee meanwhile minimizing the performance degradation compared to the ground truth. In the realm of machine learning, the mainstream technique of applying DP is Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD) [[1]( "Abadi, M., Chu, A., Goodfellow., I, McMahan., H B, Mironov., I, Talwar., K, Zhang., L: Deep learning with differential privacy. In Proceedings of the 2016 ACM SIGSAC conference on computer and communications security, pages 308–318, \(2016\)")], where the gradient is first clipped and then perturbed with Gaussian noise at each step of the optimization. Most existing DP techniques for language models are developed upon DP-SGD. Before delving into details, one caveat is DP requires a primitive definition on the ‘resolution’ of privacy preservation, that is, where does _one data entry_ (Definition [1]()) zoom into? For NLP tasks, one data entry could be data of one user (resp. user-level), a sentence (resp. sequence-level), or a word/token (resp. token-level), etc. In many cases, user-level DP is captured by local DP while the rest falls in centralized DP approaches. Apparently, various scopes of the DP concept are impactful on algorithm design and performance evaluation. We therefore include this front for each work if the context is clear.

For clarity, we will denote user-specific quantities with a superscript $( x ^ { ( j ) } )$ and record-specific quantities with a subscript $( x _ { i } )$ . We consider the setting of training a machine learning model on the data of $N$ unique users, with each user contributing up to $K$ images. The cardinality of the set of images contributed by the $j ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ user will be denoted $k ^ { ( j ) }$ . The training of the machine learning model should satisfy DP with respect to some adjacency relation on the user database. For now, we will assume an equal target privacy budget for all users, but subsequently, we want to enable users to choose their specific budget based on their individual privacy perception. For simplicity, we will specify the target user-level privacy budget in terms of approximate DP, i.e. as an $( \widetilde { \varepsilon } , \widetilde { \delta } )$ tuple. Although it is possible to assign a user-specific $( \widetilde { \varepsilon } ^ { ( j ) } , \widetilde { \delta } ^ { ( j ) } )$ , we assume the same user-specific budgets efor all users for simplicity. Moreover, sharper accounting can be achieved by specifying the budget in terms of a privacy curve or collection of RDP budgets [26–28]. Recall that our aim is to design a technique that (1) allows users to contribute an arbitrary number of samples and (2) accounts precisely for the actual number of samples rather than an upper bound on the samples ( $K$ in the notation above).

#### User-level DP:

  - Similar structure with three users contributing different shapes.
  - However, the right side shows a blue box covering User 1's data, indicating aggregation or exclusion in user-level DP.
- **Training Process:**
  - Datasets undergo "Noisy Training," treating each user's data as a group.
  - The resulting models are identical, suggesting that the privacy mechanism at the user level maintains consistency despite aggregated data handling.

#### General Observations:
- **Models:**
  - Both scenarios result in models that "look the same," highlighting the effectiveness of DP in maintaining model performance while ensuring privacy.
- **Privacy Levels:**
  - Example-level DP focuses on individual data points, while user-level DP considers entire user datasets, emphasizing different privacy granularities.

### Fine-grained Description:

- **CLIP Model Processing**:
  - **Text Encoding**: 
    - The text prompt is encoded by the CLIP model.
  - **Image Encoding**: 
    - The augmented batch images are encoded by the CLIP model.

- **Comparison and Optimization**:
  - **Cosine Distance**:
    - The encoded text and image representations are compared using cosine distance to determine their similarity.
  - **Loss Calculation**:
    - A loss value is calculated based on the cosine distance, which is used in a gradient loop to refine the image generation process.

- **Overall Workflow**:
  - The process iterates through the loop, adjusting the images to better match the text description, enhancing the accuracy and relevance of the generated images to the input text. 

- **Purpose**:
  - The diagram highlights the integration of vector rendering, image processing, and machine learning to create images that closely align with textual descriptions.

### Fine-grained Description

#### Initial Process
- **Noise Initialization**: 
  - \( x_T^1 \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I) \): The process starts with a noise vector sampled from a normal distribution.
  - \( x_T^1 = \text{DDIM\_Backward}(x_T, \Delta t, SD) \): The noise is processed using a deterministic denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) backward process.

#### Iterative Refinement
- **Loop for \( k = 2, 3, \ldots, m \)**:
  - \( \tilde{x}_T^k = W_k(x_T^1) \): The initial vector undergoes a transformation \( W_k \).
  - \( x_T^k = \text{DDPM\_Forward}(\tilde{x}_T^k, \Delta t) \): The transformed vector is then processed using a diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) forward process.

#### Convolution and Attention
- **Convolution Layer**: The vector undergoes convolution operations, depicted as multiple layers processing the input.
- **Cross-Frame Attention**:
  - **Linear Projections**: The input \( x_t^1 \) and \( x_t^k \) are projected linearly into keys (\( K^1 \)), values (\( V^1 \)), and queries (\( Q^k \)).
  - **Attention Mechanism**: These projections undergo a softmax-based attention mechanism to integrate information across frames.
  - **Transformer Blocks**: Two transformer blocks process the attended features, followed by a feed-forward network (FFN).

#### Background Smoothing
- **Salient Object Detection**:
  - \( \hat{x}_t^k = W_k(x_t^1) \): A transformation identifies salient features.
  - \( \hat{x}_t^k = M^k \odot \hat{x}_t^k + (1 - M^k) \odot (x_t^1) \): The salient object is emphasized using a mask \( M^k \), blending the transformed and original features.
- **DDIM Step**: The refined features undergo further processing using DDIM to produce the final image.

#### Text Prompt
- The text prompt "A horse is galloping on the street" guides the generation process, indicating the desired content of the output image.

#### Annotations
- The process iterates for \( k = 1, 2, \ldots, m \) and repeats for \( T \) steps, ensuring progressive refinement of the image.

The diagram illustrates a sophisticated pipeline for image generation that emphasizes salient features while smoothing the background, using a combination of diffusion models and transformer-based attention mechanisms.

[Source txt1 begin]
3.4.1 Differentially-Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD). Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD) [1] adapts SGD to satisfy $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -differential privacy by clipping per-example gradients to a fixed norm and adding Gaussian noise. This method builds on stochastic optimization methods [189] [128] [190] to ensure differential privacy in deep learning.
[Source txt1 end]

[Source txt2 begin]
A differentially private version of stochastic gradient descent useful for training ML models can be obtained by bounding the influence of any individual sample in the trained model and masking it with noise. The resulting algorithm is called DP-SGD (Abadi et al., 2016) and proceeds by iteratively updating parameters with a privatized gradient descent step. Given a sampling probability $q$ , current model parameters $\theta$ and a loss function $\ell ( \theta , \cdot )$ , the privatized gradient $g$ is obtained by first creating a mini-batch $B$ including each point in the training dataset with probability $q$ , summing the $L _ { 2 }$ -clipped gradients3 for each point in $B$ , and adding Gaussian noise with standard deviation $\sigma \cdot C$ to all coordinates of the gradient: $\begin{array} { r } { g \gets \sum _ { z \in B } \mathrm { c l i p } _ { C } \left( \nabla _ { \theta } \ell ( \theta , z ) \right) + \mathcal { N } ( 0 , C ^ { 2 } \sigma ^ { 2 } I ) } \end{array}$ . Running DP-SGD for T training steps yields an (ϵ, δ)-DP mechanism with ϵ ≈ q T log(1/δ)σ (Abadi et al., 2016) – in practice, tighter numerical bounds on $\epsilon$ are often used (Gopi et al., 2021; Google DP Team, 2022). For analytic purposes it is often useful to consider alternatives to $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -DP to capture the differences between distributions $M ( D )$ and $M ( D ^ { \prime } )$ . Renyi differential privacy (RDP) (Mironov, 2017) is one ´ such alternative often used in the context of DP-SGD. It states that the mechanism is $( \alpha , \epsilon )$ -RDP for $\alpha > 1$ and $\epsilon \geq 0$ if $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } _ { W \sim M ( D ^ { \prime } ) } \left[ \left( \frac { \mathbb { P } [ M ( D ) = W ] } { \mathbb { P } [ M ( D ^ { \prime } ) = W ] } \right) ^ { \alpha } \right] \le e ^ { ( \alpha - 1 ) \epsilon } } \end{array}$ . In particular, $T$ iterations of full-batch DP-SGD (i.e. $q = 1$ ) with noise multiplier $\sigma$ give $\textstyle \left( \alpha , { \frac { \alpha T } { 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } } } \right)$ -RDP for every $\alpha > 1$ .
[Source txt2 end]

[Source txt3 begin]
We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (DP) in order to provably safeguard all the examples contributed by each user. We study two variants of DP-SGD with: (1) example-level sampling (ELS) and per-example gradient clipping, and (2) user-level sampling (ULS) and per-user gradient clipping. We derive a novel userlevel DP accountant that allows us to compute provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS. Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. We find that ULS is significantly better in settings where either (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Notably, our focus on LLMcompatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.
[Source txt3 end]

[Source txt4 begin]

Using this, we show that while ELS can outperform ULS in specific settings, ULS generally yields better results when each user has a diverse collection of examples. We validate our findings through experiments in synthetic mean estimation and LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. Notably, our focus on LLM-compatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.

DP-SGD and Privacy Amplification: Differentiallyprivate stochastic gradient descent (DP-SGD) is a common algorithm to solve private optimization problems. The basic idea is to enforce a bounded $\ell _ { 2 }$ norm of individual gradient, and add Gaussian noise to the gradients used in SGD updates. Specifically, consider a dataset $D = \{ d _ { 1 } , \ldots , d _ { n } \}$ and an objective function of the form $\textstyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } \ell ( \theta ; d _ { i } )$ for some loss function $\ell$ . DP-SGD uses an update rule
[Source txt5 end]

[Source txt6 begin]
To remedy this issue, we consider user-level DP, which instead of guaranteeing privacy for individual samples, protects a user’s entire contribution $\textcircled { m } \geq 1$ samples). This is a more stringent but more realistic privacy desideratum. To hold, it requires that the output of our algorithm does not significantly change when changing user’s entire contribution—i.e. possibly swapping up to $m$ samples in total. We make this formal in Definition 1. Very recently, for the reasons outlined above, there has been increasing interest in user-level DP for applications such as estimating discrete distributions under user-level privacy constraints [46], PAC learning with user-level privacy [31], and bounding user contributions in ML models [4, 26]. Differentially private SQL with bounded user contributions was proposed in [59]. User-level privacy has been also studied in the context of learning models via federated learning [49, 48, 58, 6].
[Source txt6 end]

[Source txt7 begin]
Despite user-level DP being a strict privacy definition and the challenges of training large models with DP, our experiments demonstrated that fine-tuning LLMs with user-level DP is both feasible and advantageous over sticking to pre-trained models thanks to our optimizations. Our work thus enables model trainers to fine-tune their models to sensitive datasets while still providing strong protections to their users.

[Source txt8 begin]
**Abstract :** Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for tackling complex tasks across diverse domains, but they also raise privacy concerns when fine-tuned on sensitive data due to potential memorization. While differential privacy (DP) offers a promising solution by ensuring models are “almost indistinguishable” with or without any particular privacy unit, current evaluations on LLMs mostly treat each example (text record) as the privacy unit. This leads to uneven user privacy guarantees when contributions per user vary. We therefore study user-level DP motivated by applications where it is necessary to ensure uniform privacy protection across users. We present a systematic evaluation of user-level DP for LLM fine-tuning on natural language generation tasks. Focusing on two mechanisms for achieving user-level DP guarantees, Group Privacy and User-wise DP-SGD, we investigate design choices like data selection strategies and parameter tuning for the best privacy-utility tradeoff.
[Source txt8 end]

[Source txt9 begin]
Differential privacy and DP-SGD. Differential privacy (Dwork et al., 2006) formalizes the idea that data analysis algorithms whose output does not overly depend on any individual input data point can provide reasonable privacy protections. Formally, we say that a randomized mechanism $M$ satisfies $( \epsilon , \delta )$ -differential privacy (DP) if, for any two datasets $D , D ^ { \prime }$ that differ by one point, and any subset $S$ of the output space we have $P [ M ( \dot { D } ) \in S ] \leq e ^ { \epsilon } P [ M ( D ^ { \prime } ) \in S ] + \delta$ . Informally, this means that DP mechanisms bound evidence an adversary can collect (after observing the output) about whether the point where $D$ and $D ^ { \prime }$ differ was used in the analysis. For the bound to provide a meaningful protection against an adversary interested in this membership question it is necessary to take $\epsilon \approx 1$ and $\delta \leq 1 / | D |$ .
[Source txt9 end]

[Source txt10 begin]
Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for tackling complex tasks across diverse domains, but they also raise privacy concerns when fine-tuned on sensitive data due to potential memorization. While differential privacy (DP) offers a promising solution by ensuring models are “almost indistinguishable” with or without any particular privacy unit, current evaluations on LLMs mostly treat each example (text record) as the privacy unit. This leads to uneven user privacy guarantees when contributions per user vary. We therefore study user-level DP motivated by applications where it is necessary to ensure uniform privacy protection across users. We present a systematic evaluation of user-level DP for LLM fine-tuning on natural language generation tasks. Focusing on two mechanisms for achieving user-level DP guarantees, Group Privacy and User-wise DP-SGD, we investigate design choices like data selection strategies and parameter tuning for the best privacy-utility tradeoff.
[Source txt10 end]

[Source txt11 begin]
Differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DPSGD) (Song et al., 2013; Bassily et al., 2014; Abadi et al., 2016) has become state-of-the-art in training private (deep) learning models (Abadi et al., 2016; McMahan et al., 2018; Erlingsson et al., 2020; Papernot et al., 2020b; Facebook, 2020; Tramer & Boneh \` , 2021). It operates by running stochastic gradient descent (Robbins & Monro, 1951) on noisy mini-batch gradients1, with the noise calibrated such that it ensures differential privacy. The privacy analysis heavily uses tools like privacy amplification by sampling/shuffling (Kasiviswanathan et al., 2008; Bassily et al., 2014; Abadi et al., 2016; Wang et al., 2019; Zhu & Wang, 2019; Erlingsson et al., 2019; Feldman et al., 2020b) to obtain the best privacy/utility trade-offs. Such amplification tools require that each mini-batch is a perfectly (uniformly) random subset of the training data. This assumption can make practical deployment prohibitively hard, especially in the context of distributed settings like federated learning (FL) where one has little control on which subset of the training data one sees at any time (Kairouz et al., 2019; Balle et al., 2020).
[Source txt11 end]

[Source txt12 begin]
**Abstract :** We investigate practical and scalable algorithms for training large language models (LLMs) with user-level differential privacy (ULDP). We study variants of DP-SGD that use example-level sampling (ELS) and user-level sampling (ULS). We derive a novel ULDP accountant that computes provably tight privacy guarantees for ELS, and use it to show that while ELS outperforms ULS in specific settings, ULS performs better when users have diverse collections of examples. We validate our findings in realistic LLM fine-tuning tasks under fixed compute budgets. Our results show that ULS is significantly better when (1) strong privacy guarantees are required, or (2) the compute budget is large. Our focus on LLM-compatible training algorithms allows us to scale to models with hundreds of millions of parameters and datasets with hundreds of thousands of users.
[Source txt12 end]

[Source txt13 begin]
Differential Privacy provides an invaluable standard for reasoning about and quantifying privacy loss in an era of large-scale data analysis and machine learning. In machine learning, DP has been successfully adapted to a wide range of algorithms. While objective perturbation is common for convex models like SVMs, and specialized techniques exist for decision trees and ensembles, gradient perturbation via DP-SGD has become the de facto standard for deep learning. This involves careful gradient clipping, noise addition, and sophisticated privacy accounting over iterative training.
[Source txt13 end]

[Source txt14 begin]
Training With Differential Privacy. Differential privacy (DP) [13, 14] is a well-established notion of privacy that offers strong guarantees on the privacy of individual records in the training dataset. Private machine learning models can be trained with variants of the differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DP-SGD) algorithm [1] which is widely implemented [17, 25]. Large companies have even used DP in production machine learning models to protect users’ sensitive information [15,69]. The tradeoffs between privacy and utility of models have been studied extensively: differentially-private training typically prevents models from capturing the long tails of the data distribution and thus hurts utility [19, 20, 67].
[Source txt14 end]

[Source txt15 begin]
DP protects individual examples, it can fail to protect the privacy of users who contribute many examples. This highlights the need for scalable algorithms and software for fine-tuning LLMs with DP at the user-level. Currently, user-level DP algorithms have been designed for small models in federated learning, but do not yet scale to LLMs.
[Source txt15 end]

[Revisions]()[BibTeX]())[CC BY 4.0]( "Licensed under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International")
**Keywords :** differential privacy, user-level, large language models, fine-tuning
**TL;DR :** We study and compare algorithms for training large language models with user-level differential privacy.

[Source txt18 begin]
An ideal DP algorithm protects the data privacy with the given \\((\epsilon,\delta )\\) guarantee meanwhile minimizing the performance degradation compared to the ground truth. In the realm of machine learning, the mainstream technique of applying DP is Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD) [[1]( "Abadi, M., Chu, A., Goodfellow., I, McMahan., H B, Mironov., I, Talwar., K, Zhang., L: Deep learning with differential privacy. In Proceedings of the 2016 ACM SIGSAC conference on computer and communications security, pages 308–318, \(2016\)")], where the gradient is first clipped and then perturbed with Gaussian noise at each step of the optimization. Most existing DP techniques for language models are developed upon DP-SGD. Before delving into details, one caveat is DP requires a primitive definition on the ‘resolution’ of privacy preservation, that is, where does _one data entry_ (Definition [1]()) zoom into? For NLP tasks, one data entry could be data of one user (resp. user-level), a sentence (resp. sequence-level), or a word/token (resp. token-level), etc. In many cases, user-level DP is captured by local DP while the rest falls in centralized DP approaches. Apparently, various scopes of the DP concept are impactful on algorithm design and performance evaluation. We therefore include this front for each work if the context is clear.
[Source txt18 end]

[Source txt19 begin]
Thus, example-level DP can fail to adequately protect a user’s privacy from, for instance, membership inference attacks at the user-level [11, 12]. However, DP can also be instantiated at the user level to safeguard all the data contributed by a user and provably mitigate such concerns. In this work, we are interested in scalable and practical approaches to fine-tuning LLMs with user-level DP.
[Source txt19 end]

[Source txt20 begin]
For clarity, we will denote user-specific quantities with a superscript $( x ^ { ( j ) } )$ and record-specific quantities with a subscript $( x _ { i } )$ . We consider the setting of training a machine learning model on the data of $N$ unique users, with each user contributing up to $K$ images. The cardinality of the set of images contributed by the $j ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ user will be denoted $k ^ { ( j ) }$ . The training of the machine learning model should satisfy DP with respect to some adjacency relation on the user database. For now, we will assume an equal target privacy budget for all users, but subsequently, we want to enable users to choose their specific budget based on their individual privacy perception. For simplicity, we will specify the target user-level privacy budget in terms of approximate DP, i.e. as an $( \widetilde { \varepsilon } , \widetilde { \delta } )$ tuple. Although it is possible to assign a user-specific $( \widetilde { \varepsilon } ^ { ( j ) } , \widetilde { \delta } ^ { ( j ) } )$ , we assume the same user-specific budgets efor all users for simplicity. Moreover, sharper accounting can be achieved by specifying the budget in terms of a privacy curve or collection of RDP budgets [26–28]. Recall that our aim is to design a technique that (1) allows users to contribute an arbitrary number of samples and (2) accounts precisely for the actual number of samples rather than an upper bound on the samples ( $K$ in the notation above).
[Source txt20 end]
